Use only when the user explicitly mentions Herdr or asks to use Herdr to inspect or control panes, tabs, workspaces, commands, or another agent. Do not use merely because a task could benefit from a background terminal, delegation, or parallel work. Requires HERDR_ENV=1."\n---\n\n# Herdr\n\nHerdr organizes terminals into workspaces, tabs, and panes, recognizes coding agents running inside panes, and exposes the current session through the `herdr` CLI.\n\nBefore issuing any control command, verify that this agent is running inside a Herdr-managed pane:\n\n```bash\ntest "${HERDR_ENV:-}" = 1\n```\n\nIf the check fails, say that you are not running inside Herdr and stop. Do not inspect or control the focused Herdr session from outside Herdr.\n\nWhen the check passes, the `herdr` binary in `PATH` talks to the current session. Use it to inspect neighboring work, create terminal layout, start agents and commands, read output, and wait for state changes.\n\n## Learn the current CLI\n\nThe installed binary is the authority for command syntax. Start with:\n\n```bash\nherdr --help\n```\n\nThen print the relevant command group by running the group without a subcommand:\n\n```bash\nherdr agent\nherdr pane\nherdr workspace\nherdr tab\nherdr worktree\nherdr terminal\nherdr notification\nherdr integration\nherdr session\nherdr machine\n```\n\nDo not run bare `herdr` for discovery; it launches or attaches the TUI. Do not probe a mutating nested command by omitting arguments. Commands such as `herdr workspace create` are valid with defaults and will execute.\n\nMost control commands return JSON. Read identifiers and state from those responses instead of predicting them.\n\n## Understand layout, panes, and agents\n\nChoose the primitive that matches the job:\n\n- Workspace, tab, and pane topology organize terminal locations.\n- Pane commands control raw terminals, shells, tests, servers, input, and output.\n- Agent commands control the recognized coding agent currently occupying a pane.\n\nA pane exists whether or not it contains an agent. `agent start` requires an existing available shell pane and never creates, splits, or moves layout. Use pane commands for ordinary processes. Use agent commands when Herdr must validate agent identity or interpret `idle`, `working`, `blocked`, `done`, and `unknown` lifecycle states.\n\nAgent commands accept either a unique live agent name or the pane ID currently hosting that agent. They do not accept terminal IDs or bare agent-kind labels. Names must match `[a-z][a-z0-9_-]{0,31}` and be unique among live agents. A name follows the current pane occupant and is cleared when that agent exits, is released, or is replaced.\n\n`idle` and `done` both mean the agent is ready for input. The CLI/API uses the server\'s seen state to distinguish them; explicit focus commands mark the target seen, while reads do not. Each TUI client tracks viewed completions independently, so its Done badge can differ from the CLI or another client\'s badge. `blocked` means Herdr recognized an approval or question UI. `unknown` means an agent is present but Herdr cannot classify it confidently; it does not prove completion.\n\n## Use IDs and caller context\n\nPublic IDs are opaque stable handles:\n\n- workspace: `w1`\n- tab: `w1:t1`\n- pane: `w1:p1`\n\nClosed tab and pane IDs are not reused. A pane moved into another workspace receives a new workspace-qualified pane ID. After `pane move`, continue with `.result.move_result.pane.pane_id` or the live agent name. The old value is reported as `.result.move_result.previous_pane_id`; only the moved process\'s inherited caller context keeps resolving that old ID, so do not use it as a general agent target.\n\nHerdr injects the caller\'s context into each managed pane:\n\n```bash\nprintf \'%s\\n\' "$HERDR_WORKSPACE_ID" "$HERDR_TAB_ID" "$HERDR_PANE_ID"\n```\n\nPrefer `--current` when a pane command should target the calling pane. An omitted `pane split` target uses the calling pane when `HERDR_PANE_ID` is available, otherwise the focused pane. Other commands may use the UI-focused pane, which can belong to the user or another client.\n\nDiscover live state with:\n\n```bash\nherdr workspace list\nherdr tab list --workspace "$HERDR_WORKSPACE_ID"\nherdr pane current --current\nherdr pane list --workspace "$HERDR_WORKSPACE_ID"\nherdr agent list\n```\n\nCreation responses expose the IDs to use next. `workspace create` returns `.result.workspace`, `.result.tab`, and `.result.root_pane`. `tab create` returns `.result.tab` and `.result.root_pane`. `pane split` returns the new pane as `.result.pane`.\n\nIDs and live agent names are scoped to one server. Two saved SSH machines can both have `w1:p1` or an agent named `reviewer`. Selecting a machine in the TUI does not retarget commands running in your pane: without `--machine`, they still use the inherited session and socket context.\n\nTo control a saved SSH machine, use the same global prefix for discovery and every later command:\n\n```bash\nherdr --machine <label-or-id> agent list\nherdr --machine <label-or-id> pane list\nherdr --machine <label-or-id> agent prompt <remote-agent-name> "Reply with your current status." --wait --timeout 120000\n```\n\nThe selector must be an enabled saved profile ID or a unique, case-sensitive label, not an arbitrary SSH hostname. Commands use that profile\'s remote session without an open TUI. Do not combine `--machine` with `--session` or `--remote`. Discover IDs on that machine; inherited local IDs and `--current` do not identify remote panes.\n\nBoth installations must support machine API forwarding, and the remote server must already be running and API-compatible. Forwarding never installs, starts, or restarts a server and never falls back to Local. Local configuration, session management, installation commands, and interactive attachment are not forwarded. Remote worktree paths must be absolute, `~`, or start with `~/`; plugin link paths must be absolute. A connection failure does not prove a mutation was not applied: inspect remote state before retrying.\n\n`herdr machine list` lists saved connection profiles, not a cross-machine pane inventory; add `--json` for scripts. Only add, remove, enable, or disable profiles when the user asks. Removing a profile disconnects the client but does not stop remote sessions. Adding a machine uses the remote default session unless `--remote-session` is explicitly supplied. Setup asks before stopping an incompatible server and defaults to No; do not approve replacement without the user\'s consent. Experimental handoff is not part of `machine add`.\n\n## Start and coordinate an agent\n\nDefault to a sibling pane in the current tab and the current working directory. Do not create a workspace, tab, worktree, or different cwd unless the user explicitly requests that topology or location.\n\nHonor a direction requested by the user. Otherwise inspect the caller pane:\n\n```bash\nherdr pane layout --pane "$HERDR_PANE_ID"\n```\n\nSplit a wide pane to the right and a narrow or tall pane down. Avoid repeated same-direction splits that create unusably narrow columns or short rows. Keep the user\'s focus in the calling pane and explicitly preserve the caller\'s working directory:\n\n```bash\nherdr pane split --current --direction right --cwd "$PWD" --no-focus\n```\n\nReplace `right` with `down` when appropriate. Read the new pane ID from `.result.pane.pane_id`.\n\nAn available shell pane must be at its interactive prompt, with the shell itself in the foreground and no foreground command, editor, or agent running. Start a supported agent in that pane with a useful unique name:\n\n```bash\nherdr agent start reviewer --kind codex --pane <returned-pane-id>\n```\n\nUse the kind requested by the user. Run `herdr agent` to inspect the installed kind list and options. Pass native agent arguments only after `--`:\n\n```bash\nherdr agent start reviewer --kind codex --pane <returned-pane-id> -- <agent-args...>\n```\n\nA successful `agent start` returns only after Herdr detects the expected agent in the same pane and considers it ready for interactive input. If the agent is blocked during startup, the command returns `agent_not_ready` immediately but keeps the name available for `agent read` and `agent send-keys`. Wait until the agent becomes idle before prompting it. Startup defaults to a 30-second timeout.\n\nSubmit work through the agent surface:\n\n```bash\nherdr agent prompt reviewer "Review the current diff and report only actionable findings." --wait --timeout 120000\n```\n\n`agent prompt` honors the pane\'s live bracketed-paste mode and sends text followed by encoded Enter as one ordered submission. It reports successful submission only after both have been written; that alone does not prove the agent started a turn. For Codex on Windows, Herdr sends a paste boundary before Enter so submission does not depend on prompt size. It rejects an agent already waiting at an approval or question dialog with `agent_blocked` before sending any input. Inspect the blocked UI and ask the user before answering it. For normal agent work, `--wait` is enough: it waits for the first settled `idle`, `done`, or `blocked` state. Do not repeat those defaults with `--until`.\n\nWith `--wait`, a prompt sent from a non-working state must produce observed `working` or `blocked` activity. After submission, Herdr waits up to five seconds for that activity; unrelated `idle`, `done`, or session changes do not satisfy this gate. It returns `agent_prompt_stalled` if no activity is observed, or `timeout` if the caller\'s timeout expires first. The caller timeout includes submission time. Without a timeout, the settled-state wait is indefinite after activity is observed. This wait tracks lifecycle state, not an individual turn; if the agent is already working, completion of the active turn may satisfy it.\n\nUse `--until` only for a state-specific workflow, such as waiting for an already-running agent to request input:\n\n```bash\nherdr agent wait reviewer --until blocked --timeout 120000\n```\n\nWithout `--until`, standalone `agent wait` uses the same settled-state defaults as `agent prompt --wait`.\n\nUse logical keys for interactive agent UI controls:\n\n```bash\nherdr agent send-keys reviewer esc\nherdr agent send-keys reviewer ctrl+c\n```\n\nHerdr validates all keys before writing any bytes. Read the result through the resolved agent:\n\n```bash\nherdr agent get reviewer\nherdr agent read reviewer --source recent-unwrapped --lines 120\n```\n\nIf a wait fails or returns `blocked`, inspect `agent get` and `agent read` before deciding what input to send. A timeout or stalled response does not prove the prompt was never delivered; do not blindly submit it again. Use the pane surface only when raw terminal control is intentional.\n\n## Run an ordinary command in another pane\n\nCreate a sibling pane with the same geometry rule, preserve the caller\'s working directory, and keep user focus unchanged:\n\n```bash\nherdr pane split --current --direction right --cwd "$PWD" --no-focus\n```\n\nRead the new pane ID from `.result.pane.pane_id`, then run and inspect the command:\n\n```bash\nherdr pane run <returned-pane-id> "just test"\nherdr pane wait-output <returned-pane-id> --match "test result" --timeout 120000\nherdr pane read <returned-pane-id> --source recent-unwrapped --lines 120\n```\n\n`pane run` atomically sends command text and Enter. `pane wait-output` searches the selected snapshot immediately, so output that already exists can match. Use `--match <text>` for a literal substring or `--regex <pattern>` for a Rust regular expression. Omitting `--timeout` allows an indefinite wait.\n\nUse the read source that matches the task:\n\n- `visible`: the currently rendered viewport.\n- `recent`: recent rendered output, including soft wraps.\n- `recent-unwrapped`: recent output with soft wraps joined; prefer it for logs and transcripts.\n- `detection`: the plain-text bottom-buffer snapshot used for agent detection.\n\nUse `--format ansi` when colors and terminal styling are evidence. Otherwise use text.\n\n`--lines` asks Herdr for more rows from the pane\'s available screen and host scrollback. Alternate-screen rows do not enter ordinary host scrollback. For supported idle agents, Herdr can collect application-owned history and restore the viewport afterward, but not every application or response can be recovered this way.\n\nIf a larger recent read still does not reveal the completed response, ask the agent to write it as Markdown in a temporary directory and reply only with the file path, then read that file on the same machine. Use this only as a fallback; do not request file output in the initial prompt.\n\n## Safety and coordination rules\n\n- Use `--no-focus` for background work unless the user asked to switch context.\n- Use `--current`, an explicit pane ID, or a unique agent name. Do not rely on another client\'s focused pane.\n- Parse IDs from JSON responses. Do not derive them from sidebar order or examples.\n- Do not close workspaces, tabs, panes, or sessions you did not create unless the user explicitly asked. `workspace close --group` closes the primary workspace and its linked worktree workspaces; never add it merely to bypass `workspace_group_close_required`.\n- Use `--trust-repository` only after the user has verified the repository. It grants per-request Git trust; it is not a routine retry for a failed worktree command.\n- Client and server versions can differ after an update. Check `herdr status` before relying on new server features. A missing method is not permission to stop or upgrade a server.\n- Never run `herdr server stop` from an active session unless the user explicitly intends to stop the server and its pane processes.\n- Never kill the main Herdr process. Use named test sessions for experiments that need an isolated server.\n- CLI server errors are JSON on stderr with exit status 1. CLI syntax errors exit with status 2.\n\r\n'}]

So, in terms of organization, pretty clear.\r\n52-\r\n53:**0:55** · This is a lot easier to work with versus just having a bunch of terminal tabs just out in space on my desktop. tracking\ue201 L59:   * \ue200cite\ue20245†Prefix input source switching\ue201 L60:   * \ue200cite\ue20246†Environment variables\ue201 L61:   * \ue200cite\ue20247†Logs\ue201 L62: ## On this page\nL63: L79:   * \ue200cite\ue20241†Advanced configuration\ue201 L80:   * \ue200cite\ue20242†Kitty graphics\ue201 L81:   * \ue200cite\ue20243†Agent session restore\ue201 L82:   * \ue200cite\ue20244†IME cursor tracking\ue201 L83:   * \ue200cite\ue20245†Prefix input source switching\ue201 L84:   * \ue200cite\ue20246†Environment variables\ue201 L85:   * \ue200cite\ue20247†Logs\ue201 L86: # Configuration\nL87: \nL88: Herdr works without a config file. Add one when you want custom keys, themes, sidebar layouts, notifications, or advanced behavior.\nL89: \nL90: The \ue200cite\ue20216†Config reference\ue201 lists every setting and keybinding, with types, defaults, and allowed values. This page covers setup, common recipes, and configuration structures that need more explanation than a reference row.\nL91: ## Config file\nL92: \nL93: \ue200cite\ue20226†Section titled “Config file”\ue201 L94: \nL95: Herdr reads config from:\nL96: \nL97:     `\nL98: \nL99:     Linux and macOS: ~/.config/herdr/config.toml\nL100: \nL101:     Windows:          %APPDATA%\\herdr\\config.toml\nL102: \nL103:     `\nL104: \nL105: Run `herdr --help` to see the resolved config path for your system.\nL106: \nL107: Print the full default config:\nL108: \nL109: Terminal window\nL110: \nL111:     `\nL112: \nL113:     herdr --default-config\nL114: \nL115:     `\nL116: \nL117: Save it as your config if you want a complete starting point:\nL118: Terminal window\nL119: \nL120:     `\nL121: \nL122:     herdr --default-config > ~/.config/herdr/config.toml\nL123: \nL124:     `\nL125: \nL126: If a config value is invalid, Herdr falls back to a safe default and shows a startup warning.\nL127: \nL128: Herdr shows first-run setup when `onboarding` is missing or true. Continuing from onboarding writes `onboarding = false` and opens settings on the integrations tab. Set `onboarding = false` to skip that flow after setup.\nL129: \nL130:     `\nL131: \nL132:     onboarding = false\nL133: \nL134:     `\nL135: ## Reload config\nL136: \nL137: \ue200cite\ue20227†Section titled “Reload config”\ue201 L138: \nL139: Reload a running server after editing `config.toml`:\nL140: \nL141: Terminal window\nL142: \nL143:     `\nL144: \nL145:     herdr server reload-config\nL146: \nL147:     `\nL148: \nL149: You can also open the global menu in Herdr and choose `reload config`.\nL150: \nL151: Reload applies most UI settings without restarting panes. Startup-only settings still need a restart.\nL152: Themes, sidebar layouts, copy behavior, and other presentation settings come from the client’s local config, including when viewing an SSH machine. Pane defaults, worktrees, integrations, and custom commands belong to the server where the panes run. The UI’s `reload config` action reloads both the client’s local settings and the selected server’s config. Local keybindings reload too; `--remote-keybindings server` instead uses the selected server’s keybindings.\nL153: ## Headless terminal size\nL154: \nL155: \ue200cite\ue20228†Section titled “Headless terminal size”\ue201 L156: \nL157: When no client is attached, the server uses a 120×40 virtual terminal for layout and newly created panes. Change that fallback for headless orchestration with:\nL158: \nL159:     `\nL160: \nL161:     [server]\nL162: \nL163:     headless_cols = 160\nL164: \nL165:     headless_rows = 50\nL166: \nL167:     `\nL168: With one attached client, all tabs follow its size. With multiple clients, each viewed tab follows the client that most recently focused, selected, or interacted with it. When all but one client detach, all tabs immediately return to the remaining client’s size. When no clients remain, existing pane PTYs retain their last size and new headless layout uses the configured fallback.\nL169: ## Terminal defaults\nL170: \nL171: \ue200cite\ue20229†Section titled “Terminal defaults”\ue201 L172: \nL173: Set the executable Herdr uses for newly created interactive panes:\nL174: \nL175:     `\nL176: \nL177:     [terminal]\nL178: \nL179:     default_shell = "nu"\nL180: \nL181:     `\nL182: When unset or empty, Herdr uses `$SHELL`, then `/bin/sh` on Unix and PowerShell on Windows. This is an executable name or path, not a shell command line. Existing panes keep their current shell until they are recreated. Custom command keybinding strings run through `/bin/sh -c` for pane commands and `/bin/sh -lc` for detached commands on Unix; on Windows they run through `cmd.exe /d /c`.\nL183: Set how Herdr starts newly created interactive pane shells:\nL184: \nL185:     `\nL186: \nL187:     [terminal]\nL188: \nL189:     shell_mode = "auto"\nL190: \nL191:     `\nL192: `shell_mode = "auto"` starts login shells on macOS so login-only PATH setup such as `/usr/libexec/path_helper` and Homebrew shell initialization runs in new panes. On other platforms, it keeps the existing non-login shell behavior. Use `"login"` to force login-shell startup or `"non_login"` to force non-login startup. Command panes, detached custom command keybindings, and explicit argv launches keep their existing command execution paths.\nL193: Set the working directory policy for new panes, tabs, and workspaces:\nL194: \nL195:     `\nL196: \nL197:     [terminal]\nL198: \nL199:     new_cwd = "follow"\nL200: \nL201:     `\nL202: \nL203: `new_cwd = "follow"` keeps the default behavior and inherits the source pane or workspace. When there is no source workspace, Herdr starts in `$HOME`. Use `"home"` to always start in `$HOME`, `"current"` to use Herdr’s process directory, or a fixed path such as `"~/Projects"`. Explicit `--cwd` values from the CLI or socket API still take precedence.\nL204: ## Worktrees\nL205: \nL206: \ue200cite\ue20230†Section titled “Worktrees”\ue201 L207: \nL208: Set the root directory Herdr uses for Git worktree checkouts created from the sidebar:\nL209: \nL210:     `\nL211: \nL212:     [worktrees]\nL213: \nL214:     directory = "~/.herdr/worktrees"\nL215: \nL216:     `\nL217: \nL218: Herdr creates checkouts under `<directory>/<repo>/<branch-slug>`. For sibling-style checkouts, set this to a directory such as `~/Projects/herdr-worktrees`. Relative values are resolved to an absolute path when the app applies the config.\nL219: Worktree actions are available from Git workspace rows. `New worktree` creates a checkout. It checks out an existing local branch when the entered branch exists; otherwise, it creates the branch. It then opens the checkout as a new Herdr workspace and groups it under the source workspace. `Open worktree...` lists existing Git worktree checkouts for that repo. Choosing an already-open checkout focuses it, while choosing a closed checkout opens it in the same group.\nL220: Grouped worktrees still behave like normal Herdr workspaces: they can be focused, renamed, closed, and contain their own tabs and panes. The parent row is the original workspace. Closing the parent row closes the whole Herdr group, but it does not delete checkout folders or branches.\nL221: To delete a worktree checkout, use `Delete worktree checkout...` on a grouped child workspace. Herdr runs `git worktree remove`, first asking Git to remove safely. If Git refuses because the checkout has modified or untracked files, Herdr asks again before running the forced remove. Branches are not deleted.\nL222: ## Remote attach\nL223: \nL224: \ue200cite\ue20231†Section titled “Remote attach”\ue201 L225: \nL226: Remote attach manages its SSH connection with temporary keepalives and, where supported, connection reuse by default.\nL227: \nL228:     `\nL229: \nL230:     [remote]\nL231: \nL232:     manage_ssh_config = true\nL233: \nL234:     `\nL235: When enabled, `herdr --remote` writes a private temporary SSH config that includes your user and system SSH configs first, then adds fallback `ServerAliveInterval` and `ServerAliveCountMax` values. Your own keepalive settings win. Linux and macOS clients also use a private per-attach OpenSSH control socket to reuse the first authenticated connection; Windows OpenSSH does not. Set `manage_ssh_config = false` to run remote attach through plain `ssh` without Herdr’s generated config or control socket.\nL236: ## Keybindings\nL237: \nL238: \ue200cite\ue20232†Section titled “Keybindings”\ue201 L239: \nL240: For a guided introduction to the prefix and a vetted prefix-free setup, see \ue200cite\ue2028†Keyboard\ue201 .\nL241: \nL242: Herdr has a prefix mode similar to tmux. The default prefix is `ctrl+b`. Keybinding strings are explicit: `prefix+n` means press the configured prefix and then `n`; `ctrl+alt+n` is a direct terminal-mode shortcut.\nL243: A small keybinding override looks like this:\nL244: \nL245:     `\nL246: \nL247:     [keys]\nL248: \nL249:     prefix = "ctrl+b"\nL250: \nL251:     goto = "prefix+g"\nL252: \nL253:     new_tab = "prefix+c"\nL254: \nL255:     next_tab = "prefix+n"\nL256: \nL257:     previous_tab = "prefix+p"\nL258: \nL259:     focus_pane_left = "prefix+h"\nL260: \nL261:     navigate_workspace_down = "j"\nL262: \nL263:     navigate_pane_down = "ctrl+j"\nL264: \nL265:     split_horizontal = "prefix+minus"\nL266: \nL267:     `\nL268: The default keymap is prefix-first so Herdr does not steal input from shells, editors, tmux, or terminal apps. Search `keys.` in the \ue200cite\ue20216†Config reference\ue201 to see every action and default binding. The in-app help panel at `prefix+?` shows the active bindings.\nL269: \nL270: A binding may also be an array when one action needs multiple shortcuts:\nL271: \nL272:     `\nL273: \nL274:     [keys]\nL275: \nL276:     next_tab = ["prefix+n", "ctrl+alt+]"]\nL277: \nL278:     `\nL279: Optional actions are unset by default. Bind them with `prefix+` for prefix-mode behavior, or use an explicit modified chord when you intentionally want a direct shortcut. For example, tmux-style one-keystroke pane resizing without entering resize mode:\nL280: \nL281:     `\nL282: \nL283:     [keys]\nL284: \nL285:     resize_pane_left = "ctrl+shift+alt+left"\nL286: \nL287:     resize_pane_down = "ctrl+shift+alt+down"\nL288: \nL289:     resize_pane_up = "ctrl+shift+alt+up"\nL290: \nL291:     resize_pane_right = "ctrl+shift+alt+right"\nL292: \nL293:     `\nL294: Key strings accept plain keys, modifier combinations such as `ctrl+a`, `shift+n`, `alt+1`, `cmd+k`, and special keys such as `enter`, `tab`, `esc`, `left`, `right`, `up`, and `down`. Named punctuation such as `minus`, `comma`, `ampersand`, `plus`, and `backtick` is also accepted. Plain direct printable keys such as `n` are unsafe because they intercept typing; use `prefix+n` unless you intentionally want a direct binding.\nL295: The `navigate_workspace_*` and `navigate_pane_*` fields are navigate-mode-only and may use plain keys such as `j` or `k`; they must not use `prefix+`, `esc`, `enter`, `tab`, `shift+tab`, `left`, `right`, or unmodified `1` through `9`. Left and right arrows are permanent aliases for pane-left and pane-right navigation.\nL296: These navigate-mode shortcuts are independent from general action bindings such as `focus_pane_down = "prefix+j"`; when both use the same key, the navigate-mode shortcut wins while navigate mode is open. Alt, Cmd/Super, and punctuation with modifiers depend on your terminal and tmux settings.\nL297: If you have old custom keybindings and want the new defaults, run `herdr config reset-keys`. Herdr backs up `config.toml`, removes `[keys]` and `[[keys.command]]`, and uses built-in v2 defaults after restart or `herdr server reload-config`.\nL298: ## Indexed jumps\nL299: \nL300: \ue200cite\ue20233†Section titled “Indexed jumps”\ue201 L301: \nL302: Indexed keybindings use `1..9` in normal keybinding fields:\nL303: \nL304:     `\nL305: \nL306:     [keys]\nL307: \nL308:     switch_tab = "prefix+1..9"\nL309: \nL310:     switch_workspace = "prefix+shift+1..9"\nL311: \nL312:     focus_agent = "prefix+alt+1..9"\nL313: \nL314:     `\nL315: \nL316: The legacy `[keys.indexed]` table is still parsed for compatibility, but new configs should prefer the explicit action fields.\nL317: ## Custom command keybindings\nL318: \nL319: \ue200cite\ue20234†Section titled “Custom command keybindings”\ue201 L320: \nL321: Custom commands use the same keybinding syntax.\nL322: \nL323:     `\nL324: \nL325:     [[keys.command]]\nL326: \nL327:     key = "prefix+alt+g"\nL328: \nL329:     type = "popup"\nL330: \nL331:     command = "lazygit"\nL332: \nL333:     description = "run lazygit"\nL334: \nL335:     width = "80%"\nL336: \nL337:     height = "80%"\nL338: \nL339:     `\nL340: `type = "popup"` opens a session-modal popup without changing the tab layout. The popup receives all terminal input, including Escape, until its command exits. `width` and `height` are optional; omit them for the default half-size popup, use numbers for terminal cells, or use strings like `"80%"` for a percentage of the terminal area. Dimensions include the popup border, and values smaller than the popup minimum are clamped.\nL341: Popup commands do not receive `HERDR_PANE_ID`; use `HERDR_ACTIVE_PANE_ID` for the underlying tiled pane.\nL342: On Unix and macOS, a popup command can also provide an ad-hoc terminal without adding a split or tab:\nL343: \nL344:     `\nL345: \nL346:     [[keys.command]]\nL347: \nL348:     key = "prefix+t"\nL349: \nL350:     type = "popup"\nL351: \nL352:     command = "exec \\"${SHELL:-sh}\\""\nL353: \nL354:     description = "open scratch terminal"\nL355: \nL356:     width = "80%"\nL357: \nL358:     height = "80%"\nL359: \nL360:     `\nL361: \nL362: On Windows, use a shell command such as `command = "powershell.exe -NoLogo"` instead. Exit the shell to close the popup and restore the tiled terminal view.\nL363: `type = "pane"` opens a temporary zoomed pane and closes it when the command exits.\nL364: \nL365: `type = "shell"` runs detached in the background.\nL366: \nL367: `type = "plugin_action"` invokes an installed plugin action id. Use the qualified id when action ids are not globally unique:\nL368: \nL369:     `\nL370: \nL371:     [[keys.command]]\nL372: \nL373:     key = "prefix+l"\nL374: \nL375:     type = "plugin_action"\nL376: \nL377:     command = "example.layout.apply"\nL378: \nL379:     description = "apply layout"\nL380: \nL381:     `\nL382: `description` is optional. When set, it appears in the keybind help panel (opened with `prefix+?`) instead of the default `\'custom command\'` label.\nL383: \nL384: Custom commands receive `HERDR_SOCKET_PATH`, `HERDR_BIN_PATH`, `HERDR_ACTIVE_WORKSPACE_ID`, `HERDR_ACTIVE_TAB_ID`, `HERDR_ACTIVE_PANE_ID`, and `HERDR_ACTIVE_PANE_CWD` when those values are available. Shell commands run from the focused pane’s working directory when Herdr can detect it.\nL385: On Windows, custom command strings use `cmd.exe /d /c`, so environment variables use `%HERDR_BIN_PATH%` syntax. To run PowerShell syntax, invoke it explicitly, for example `powershell.exe -NoProfile -Command "..."`.\nL386: ## Theme\nL387: \nL388: \ue200cite\ue20235†Section titled “Theme”\ue201 L389: \nL390: Choose a built-in theme:\nL391: \nL392:     `\nL393: \nL394:     [theme]\nL395: \nL396:     name = "catppuccin"\nL397: \nL398:     `\nL399: \nL400: Search `theme.name` in the \ue200cite\ue20216†Config reference\ue201 for every built-in theme. Use `terminal` when you want Herdr UI colors to follow your host terminal’s ANSI palette.\nL401: To let Herdr switch its own UI theme when the host terminal reports a light/dark appearance change, enable theme auto-switching:\nL402: \nL403:     `\nL404: \nL405:     [theme]\nL406: \nL407:     name = "catppuccin"\nL408: \nL409:     auto_switch = true\nL410: \nL411:     light_name = "catppuccin-latte"\nL412: \nL413:     dark_name = "catppuccin"\nL414: \nL415:     `\nL416: `auto_switch` defaults to `false`, so existing theme configs keep manual behavior. If `light_name` or `dark_name` is omitted, Herdr uses the matching built-in sibling for the configured `name` when one exists, such as `tokyo-night`/`tokyo-night-day` or `gruvbox`/`gruvbox-light`. Manual theme selection in Settings disables `auto_switch`.\nL417: You can override individual colors:\nL418: \nL419:     `\nL420: \nL421:     [theme.custom]\nL422: \nL423:     sidebar_bg = "#181825"\nL424: \nL425:     active_row_bg = "#1e1e2e"\nL426: \nL427:     selection_bg = "#313244"\nL428: \nL429:     panel_bg = "reset"\nL430: \nL431:     accent = "#a6e3a1"\nL432: \nL433:     green = "#a6e3a1"\nL434: \nL435:     blue = "#89b4fa"\nL436: \nL437:     red = "#f38ba8"\nL438: \nL439:     yellow = "#f9e2af"\nL440: \nL441:     `\nL442: `sidebar_bg` optionally gives the desktop sidebar its own background. When omitted, the sidebar keeps the host terminal background. `active_row_bg` changes the active Space and focused Agent row background without affecting separators or scrollbar tracks. `selection_bg` changes the Navigate-mode cursor row background in the sidebar.\nL443: \nL444: Color values accept hex, named colors, `rgb(r,g,b)`, or reset aliases like `reset`, `default`, `none`, and `transparent`.\nL445: When `auto_switch` is enabled, optional light and dark subtables layer on top of the shared custom colors:\nL446: \nL447:     `\nL448: \nL449:     [theme.custom]\nL450: \nL451:     accent = "#89b4fa"\nL452: \nL453: \nL454: \nL455: \nL456:     [theme.custom.light]\nL457: \nL458:     panel_bg = "#eff1f5"\nL459: \nL460:     text = "#4c4f69"\nL461: \nL462: \nL463: \nL464: \nL465:     [theme.custom.dark]\nL466: \nL467:     panel_bg = "#1e1e2e"\nL468: \nL469:     text = "#cdd6f4"\nL470: \nL471:     `\nL472: The active palette is applied in this order: built-in theme, `[theme.custom]`, then `[theme.custom.light]` or `[theme.custom.dark]`. Omitting the mode subtables preserves the existing shared override behavior.\nL473: ## UI and sidebar\nL474: \nL475: \ue200cite\ue20236†Section titled “UI and sidebar”\ue201 L476: \nL477: The sidebar is the main Herdr dashboard. Search `ui.` in the \ue200cite\ue20216†Config reference\ue201 for sizing, collapsed mode, Agent panel ordering, mouse behavior, pane borders, and other presentation settings.\nL478: `ui.pane_borders` accepts `"auto"` (the default, borders only for split panes), `"always"` (also frame a single pane), or `"off"`. A single-pane frame requires `ui.pane_outer_borders = true`, because all its edges are outer edges. Existing boolean values remain valid: `true` means `"auto"` and `false` means `"off"`.\nL479: Set `tab_bar_position = "bottom"` under `[ui]` to place the desktop tab row below the terminal panes. Prefix, Navigate, Copy, and Resize mode bars temporarily replace the bottom tab row while active. The default is `"top"`.\nL480: Configure an ordered tmux-style status area at the right edge of the tab row:\nL481: \nL482:     `\nL483: \nL484:     [ui]\nL485: \nL486:     tab_bar_right = [\nL487: \nL488:       { type = "zoom" },\nL489: \nL490:       { type = "hostname" },\nL491: \nL492:       { type = "datetime", format = "%H:%M" },\nL493: \nL494:       { type = "text", text = "prod" },\nL495: \nL496:       { type = "command", command = "~/.config/herdr/status.sh", interval_seconds = 5, timeout_seconds = 2 },\nL497: \nL498:     ]\nL499: \nL500:     tab_bar_right_separator = " · "\nL501: \nL502:     `\nL503: The status area is empty by default. Add `zoom` to show a fixed `ZOOM` pill while the active tab is zoomed; the existing per-tab `Z` markers remain independent. `hostname`, `datetime`, and `command` resolve on the Herdr server, so `herdr --remote` shows the remote machine’s values. Datetime entries use `strftime` formatting; directives that require a UTC offset or Unix timestamp, such as `%z` and `%s`, are rejected because the value is server-local wall-clock time.\nL504: Command entries run immediately and then at `interval_seconds` without blocking rendering or overlapping a previous run. The interval can be 1–31,536,000 seconds and the timeout can be 1–3,600 seconds.\nL505: Herdr uses the last line of successful output, removes ESC-prefixed terminal control sequences instead of interpreting styles, clears the entry after failure, empty output, or `timeout_seconds`, and provides the same active workspace, tab, pane, socket, binary, and working-directory context as custom command keybindings. Commands are supported on Linux, macOS, and Windows, using `/bin/sh -lc` on Linux and macOS and `cmd.exe /d /c` on Windows.\nL506: Separators appear only between visible entries. Set `tab_bar_right_separator = ""` for direct concatenation. On a narrow tab row, the complete status area yields to the tabs and their controls.\nL507: ### Outer terminal window title\nL508: \nL509: \ue200cite\ue20237†Section titled “Outer terminal window title”\ue201 L510: \nL511: Herdr emulates the terminals in its panes, so an `OSC 0`/`OSC 2` title written inside a pane stops at Herdr. Herdr writes its own title to the terminal it runs in, which is what window managers and terminal tab bars read:\nL512: \nL513:     `\nL514: \nL515:     [ui]\nL516: \nL517:     window_title = "{hostname}: {workspace}"\nL518: \nL519:     `\nL520: Tokens are `{hostname}`, `{workspace}`, `{tab}`, `{pane}` (the focused pane’s manual name), and `{terminal_title}` (the focused pane’s own terminal title with spinner frames stripped). Write `{{` and `}}` for literal braces. A token with no value renders empty.\nL521: \nL522: The title renders on the Herdr server, so `{hostname}` names the machine the panes run on, including when you attach with `herdr --remote` or run `herdr` over SSH. Set `window_title = ""` to leave the outer terminal title alone.\nL523: `client.window_title.set` overrides the configured title until `client.window_title.clear` hands it back.\nL524: \nL525: Agent status uses compact colored dots by default. To distinguish blocked, working, done, idle, and unknown states by shape as well as color, choose distinct symbols in Settings or configure:\nL526: \nL527:     `\nL528: \nL529:     [ui]\nL530: \nL531:     status_indicators = "symbols"\nL532: \nL533:     `\nL534: \nL535: The symbols are static, so this option does not enable spinner animation.\nL536: ### Sidebar row layouts\nL537: \nL538: \ue200cite\ue20238†Section titled “Sidebar row layouts”\ue201 L539: The expanded desktop sidebar renders each inner array in `rows` as one line. These are the complete default layouts:\nL540: \nL541:     `\nL542: \nL543:     [ui.sidebar.agents]\nL544: \nL545:     row_gap = 0\nL546: \nL547:     rows = [\nL548: \nL549:       ["state_icon", "machine", "workspace", "tab"],\nL550: \nL551:       ["agent"],\nL552: \nL553:     ]\nL554: \nL555: \nL556: \nL557: \nL558:     [ui.sidebar.spaces]\nL559: \nL560:     row_gap = 0\nL561: \nL562:     rows = [\nL563: \nL564:       ["state_icon", "workspace"],\nL565: \nL566:       ["branch", "git_status"],\nL567: \nL568:     ]\nL569: \nL570:     `\nL571: \nL572: Agent rows accept these built-in tokens:\nL573:   * `state_icon` — colored icon for the agent’s semantic state.\nL574:   * `state_text` — `idle`, `working`, `blocked`, `done`, or `unknown`, including a reported display label when present.\nL575:   * `machine` — machine label when the client has multiple machines; omitted for a single local machine.\nL576:   * `workspace` — workspace name.\nL577:   * `tab` — tab name when available.\nL578:   * `pane` — pane name when available.\nL579:   * `agent` — detected or reported agent display name.\nL580:   * `terminal_title` — latest OSC 0/2 terminal title after safety normalization.\nL581:   * `terminal_title_stripped` — the terminal title with one recognized leading activity or spinner glyph and its following whitespace removed.\nL582:   * `$name` — custom pane metadata named `name`.\nL583: Space rows accept these built-in tokens:\nL584: \nL585:   * `state_icon` — colored icon for the space’s rolled-up agent state.\nL586:   * `state_text` — text for the rolled-up agent state.\nL587:   * `workspace` — workspace name.\nL588:   * `branch` — Git branch when available.\nL589:   * `git_status` — Git ahead and behind counts when nonzero.\nL590:   * `$name` — custom workspace metadata named `name`.\nL591: Tokens render in their configured order. Herdr normally separates adjacent values with `·` and uses a single space after `state_icon`. Missing values and their separators disappear; a row disappears when none of its tokens have a value. Existing custom rows remain unchanged, so add `machine` explicitly if you want machine identity in a custom multi-machine layout. Each layout may contain at most 16 rows, with at most 16 tokens in each row.\nL592: A token entry can also be an inline style table:\nL593: \nL594:     `\nL595: \nL596:     [ui.sidebar.agents]\nL597: \nL598:     rows = [\nL599: \nL600:       ["state_icon", { token = "workspace", bold = false }, "tab"],\nL601: \nL602:       [{ token = "$summary", fg = "#89b4fa", bold = true, dim = false }],\nL603: \nL604:     ]\nL605: \nL606:     `\nL607: `fg` accepts strict `#RGB` or `#RRGGBB`. `bold` and `dim` accept booleans. Omitted fields preserve the token’s contextual style; explicit `false` removes that modifier. Styling applies to one occurrence, so the same token may look different in another row or agent override. A foreground override replaces all semantic foregrounds inside that occurrence: for example, styled `git_status` ahead and behind counts use one color instead of their default green and red.\nL608: Token styles never change separators or row backgrounds.\nL609: Text-valued tokens also accept up to 16 ordered `rules`. Each rule contains exactly one condition—`equals`, `contains`, `starts_with`, `gt`, or `lt`—and optional `fg`, `bold`, and `dim` overrides:\nL610: \nL611:     `\nL612: \nL613:     [ui.sidebar.agents]\nL614: \nL615:     rows = [\nL616: \nL617:       ["state_icon", "workspace", "tab"],\nL618: \nL619:       [{ token = "machine", fg = "#fff", rules = [{ equals = "Local", fg = "#f55" }, { equals = "Fedora", ignore_case = true, fg = "#51a2da" }] }, "agent"],\nL620:       [{ token = "$load", fg = "#fff", rules = [{ gt = 80, fg = "#f55", bold = true }, { gt = 50, fg = "#fc0" }] }],\nL621: \nL622:     ]\nL623: \nL624:     `\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nIntegrations | herdr (https://herdr.dev/docs/integrations/)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn5view1\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({"ref_id":"https://herdr.dev/docs/integrations/","lineno":null}); Total lines: 631\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nSession state and restore | herdr (https://herdr.dev/docs/session-state/)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn5view2\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({"ref_id":"https://herdr.dev/docs/session-state/","lineno":null}); Total lines: 184\n'}]

On this page\nL65:   * \ue200cite\ue2020†Overview\ue201 L66:   * \ue200cite\ue20226†Install integrations\ue201 L67:   * \ue200cite\ue20227†Uninstall integrations\ue201 L68:   * \ue200cite\ue20228†How Herdr uses integrations\ue201 L69:   * \ue200cite\ue20229†Integrate your own agent\ue201 L70:   * \ue200cite\ue20230†Pi\ue201 L71:   * \ue200cite\ue20231†OMP\ue201 L72:   * \ue200cite\ue20232†Claude Code\ue201 L73:   * \ue200cite\ue20233†Codex\ue201 L74:   * \ue200cite\ue20234†GitHub Copilot CLI\ue201 L75:   * \ue200cite\ue20235†Devin CLI\ue201 L76:   * \ue200cite\ue20236†Kimi Code CLI\ue201 L77:   * \ue200cite\ue20237†Droid\ue201 L78:   * \ue200cite\ue20238†OpenCode\ue201 L79:   * \ue200cite\ue20239†Kilo Code CLI\ue201 L80:   * \ue200cite\ue20240†Hermes Agent\ue201 L81:   * \ue200cite\ue20241†Qoder CLI\ue201 L82:   * \ue200cite\ue20242†Qwen Code\ue201 L83:   * \ue200cite\ue20243†Letta Code\ue201 L84:   * \ue200cite\ue20244†Cursor Agent CLI\ue201 L85:   * \ue200cite\ue20245†MastraCode\ue201 L86:   * \ue200cite\ue20246†Antigravity CLI\ue201 L87:   * \ue200cite\ue20247†Grok CLI\ue201 L88:   * \ue200cite\ue20248†Custom status labels\ue201 L89:   * \ue200cite\ue20249†Debug integration state\ue201 L90: # Integrations\nL91: \nL92: Herdr detects supported agents automatically. Install official integrations when you want native agent session restore, direct lifecycle reports, or both. See \ue200cite\ue20210†Agents\ue201 for the full status authority model.\nL93: ## Install integrations\nL94: \nL95: \ue200cite\ue20226†Section titled “Install integrations”\ue201 L96: \nL97: Open settings inside Herdr and use the integrations tab to install recommended integrations for agents found on your `PATH`, or run commands manually:\nL98: Terminal window\nL99: \nL100:     `\nL101: \nL102:     herdr integration install pi\nL103: \nL104:     herdr integration install omp\nL105: \nL106:     herdr integration install claude\nL107: \nL108:     herdr integration install codex\nL109: \nL110:     herdr integration install copilot\nL111: \nL112:     herdr integration install devin\nL113: \nL114:     herdr integration install droid\nL115: \nL116:     herdr integration install kimi\nL117: \nL118:     herdr integration install opencode\nL119: \nL120:     herdr integration install kilo\nL121: \nL122:     herdr integration install hermes\nL123:     herdr integration install qodercli\nL124: \nL125:     herdr integration install qwen\nL126: \nL127:     herdr integration install letta\nL128: \nL129:     herdr integration install cursor\nL130: \nL131:     herdr integration install mastracode\nL132: \nL133:     herdr integration install antigravity-cli\nL134: \nL135:     herdr integration install grok\nL136: \nL137:     `\nL138: ## Uninstall integrations\nL139: \nL140: \ue200cite\ue20227†Section titled “Uninstall integrations”\ue201 L141: Terminal window\nL142: \nL143:     `\nL144: \nL145:     herdr integration uninstall pi\nL146: \nL147:     herdr integration uninstall omp\nL148: \nL149:     herdr integration uninstall claude\nL150: \nL151:     herdr integration uninstall codex\nL152: \nL153:     herdr integration uninstall copilot\nL154: \nL155:     herdr integration uninstall devin\nL156: \nL157:     herdr integration uninstall droid\nL158: \nL159:     herdr integration uninstall kimi\nL160: \nL161:     herdr integration uninstall opencode\nL162: \nL163:     herdr integration uninstall kilo\nL164: \nL165:     herdr integration uninstall hermes\nL166:     herdr integration uninstall qodercli\nL167: \nL168:     herdr integration uninstall qwen\nL169: \nL170:     herdr integration uninstall letta\nL171: \nL172:     herdr integration uninstall cursor\nL173: \nL174:     herdr integration uninstall mastracode\nL175: \nL176:     herdr integration uninstall antigravity-cli\nL177: \nL178:     herdr integration uninstall grok\nL179: \nL180:     `\nL181: Herdr saves integration configs atomically on Linux/macOS and for new files; existing Windows files keep their permissions through a backup-first, in-place update.\nL182: If Windows reports a backup, close the agent and inspect the config before retrying: discard—not restore—an unfinished `.herdr-backup.pending` only after verifying the config, or restore completed `.herdr-backup` contents into the existing file if needed (if missing, establish its intended permissions first), then verify the config and remove the backup.\nL183: ## How Herdr uses integrations\nL184: \nL185: \ue200cite\ue20228†Section titled “How Herdr uses integrations”\ue201 L186: \nL187: Herdr uses integrations in two ways:\nL188: Integration type  | Agents  | Effect\nL189: --- | --- | ---\nL190: Lifecycle authority  | Pi, OMP, Kimi Code CLI, OpenCode, Kilo Code CLI, MastraCode  | When installed and actively reporting for the pane, hook or plugin events author `idle`, `working`, and `blocked`. Herdr does not also use screen manifest fallback for that same lifecycle authority.\nL191: Session identity  | Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot CLI, Devin CLI, Droid, Qoder CLI, Qwen Code, Letta Code, Cursor Agent CLI, Hermes Agent, Antigravity CLI, Grok CLI  | The integration reports native session references for restore. State still comes from Herdr’s screen manifest detection.\nL192: Custom integrations can also report state that is not visible in the native terminal UI. They do not need to be built into Herdr or use a recognized agent executable.\nL193: ## Integrate your own agent\nL194: \nL195: \ue200cite\ue20229†Section titled “Integrate your own agent”\ue201 L196: \nL197: An agent running in a Herdr pane inherits `HERDR_ENV`, `HERDR_PANE_ID`, `HERDR_BIN_PATH`, and `HERDR_SOCKET_PATH`. If the agent exposes lifecycle hooks, use those hooks to report semantic state through Herdr’s CLI:\nL198: \nL199: Terminal window\nL200: \nL201:     `\nL202: \nL203:     "$HERDR_BIN_PATH" pane report-agent "$HERDR_PANE_ID" \\\nL204: \nL205:       --source custom:my-agent \\\nL206: \nL207:       --agent my-agent \\\nL208: \nL209:       --state working\nL210: \nL211:     `\nL212: Report `idle` when the agent is ready for input and `blocked` when it needs a user decision. Use `--message` to describe a block. When the agent exits, release the same source’s lifecycle authority:\nL213: \nL214: Terminal window\nL215: \nL216:     `\nL217: \nL218:     "$HERDR_BIN_PATH" pane release-agent "$HERDR_PANE_ID" \\\nL219: \nL220:       --source custom:my-agent \\\nL221: \nL222:       --agent my-agent\nL223: \nL224:     `\nL225: Report only when `HERDR_ENV=1` and the required variables are present. This keeps the integration a no-op outside Herdr. Keep `--source` stable and unique to the integration. If reports can arrive out of order, include a strictly increasing `--seq`; Herdr ignores stale sequence numbers from the same source.\nL226: You can include `--agent-session-id` or `--agent-session-path` with `report-agent`, or use `pane report-agent-session` when session identity changes independently of state. Herdr exposes that reference through its pane and agent APIs. Automatic session restore also requires Herdr to know how to launch that agent and resume the referenced session.\nL227: Use `HERDR_BIN_PATH` and the CLI wrappers for portable integrations. Code that needs direct IPC can send the equivalent `pane.report_agent`, `pane.report_agent_session`, and `pane.release_agent` requests described in the \ue200cite\ue20250†Socket API\ue201 .\nL228: \nL229: \ue200cite\ue20251†Prime Agent’s built-in Herdr reporter†github.com\ue201 is a real-world example. It activates only inside Herdr, maps agent events to `working`, `idle`, and `blocked`, preserves report ordering across sessions, and releases authority on exit.\nL230: Some integrations report native agent session references. Herdr uses official session references to resume Claude Code, Codex, Devin CLI, Droid, Kimi Code CLI, Qoder CLI, Qwen Code, Letta Code, Cursor Agent CLI, Grok CLI, GitHub Copilot CLI, Pi, OMP, Hermes Agent, OpenCode, Kilo Code CLI, MastraCode, and Antigravity CLI panes after a Herdr server restart unless `[session] resume_agents_on_restore = false` disables it.\nL231: Native session restore requires current Herdr integrations: Pi integration version `2`, OMP version `3`, Claude Code version `6`, Codex version `5`, GitHub Copilot CLI version `2`, Devin CLI version `2`, Droid version `2`, Kimi Code CLI version `3`, Qoder CLI version `2`, Qwen Code version `1`, Letta Code version `1`, Cursor Agent CLI version `1`, Grok CLI version `2`, OpenCode version `5`, Kilo Code CLI version `1`, Hermes Agent version `5`, MastraCode version `1`, or Antigravity CLI version `1`.\nL232: Check installed versions with `herdr integration status`.\nL233: ## Pi\nL234: \nL235: \ue200cite\ue20230†Section titled “Pi”\ue201 L236: \nL237: Install the Pi integration:\nL238: \nL239: Terminal window\nL240: \nL241:     `\nL242: \nL243:     herdr integration install pi\nL244: \nL245:     `\nL246: \nL247: Herdr writes the bundled extension to:\nL248: \nL249:     `\nL250: \nL251:     ~/.pi/agent/extensions/herdr-agent-state.ts\nL252: \nL253:     `\nL254: \nL255: If `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR` is set, Herdr writes to `$PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR/extensions/herdr-agent-state.ts` instead. Herdr creates the extensions directory when the Pi agent directory already exists. Uninstall removes only that extension file.\nL256: ## OMP\nL257: \nL258: \ue200cite\ue20231†Section titled “OMP”\ue201 L259: \nL260: Install the OMP integration:\nL261: \nL262: Terminal window\nL263: \nL264:     `\nL265: \nL266:     herdr integration install omp\nL267: \nL268:     `\nL269: \nL270: Herdr writes the bundled extension to:\nL271: \nL272:     `\nL273: \nL274:     ~/.omp/agent/extensions/herdr-omp-agent-state.ts\nL275: \nL276:     `\nL277: Herdr uses `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR` as the complete agent directory when set. Otherwise, it uses `$HOME/$PI_CONFIG_DIR/agent` when `PI_CONFIG_DIR` is set, falling back to `~/.omp/agent`. If Pi and OMP resolve to the same extension directory, Herdr refuses the OMP install so the OMP extension cannot be loaded by Pi. Configure separate agent directories before installing both integrations. Herdr creates the extensions directory when the resolved OMP agent directory already exists.\nL278: Uninstall removes only that extension file.\nL279: The OMP integration reports `omp` as the agent label, lifecycle state, and native session identity through Herdr’s socket API. It does not require native process detection for the `omp` executable, and Herdr can resume an OMP pane with `omp --resume=<session>` after a server restart.\nL280: ## Claude Code\nL281: \nL282: \ue200cite\ue20232†Section titled “Claude Code”\ue201 L283: \nL284: Install the Claude Code hook:\nL285: \nL286: Terminal window\nL287: \nL288:     `\nL289: \nL290:     herdr integration install claude\nL291: \nL292:     `\nL293: \nL294: The hook reports Claude Code session identity to the local Herdr socket on session start. Claude Code state comes from Herdr’s screen manifest detection.\nL295: Claude integration v10 matches only the documented `SessionStart` sources: `startup`, `resume`, `clear`, `compact`, and `fork`. This prevents Grok’s `new` and `load` events from launching the imported Herdr Claude hook, without disabling unrelated Claude-compatible hooks. Reinstall the Claude integration after upgrading to migrate the previous wildcard matcher. New Claude source values require an integration update.\nL296: Herdr uses `~/.claude` by default, or `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` when set. The Claude config directory must already exist. Install writes `hooks/herdr-agent-state.sh` and updates `settings.json` with Herdr hook entries. Uninstall removes the matching hook entries and deletes the hook script.\nL297: ## Codex\nL298: \nL299: \ue200cite\ue20233†Section titled “Codex”\ue201 L300: \nL301: Install the Codex hook:\nL302: \nL303: Terminal window\nL304: \nL305:     `\nL306: \nL307:     herdr integration install codex\nL308: \nL309:     `\nL310: \nL311: The Codex hook reports session identity through the same local socket API used by other integrations. Codex state comes from Herdr’s screen manifest detection.\nL312: Herdr uses `~/.codex` by default, or `CODEX_HOME` when set. The Codex config directory must already exist. Install writes `herdr-agent-state.sh`, updates `hooks.json`, and ensures `[features] hooks = true` in `config.toml`. It also removes the deprecated top-level `codex_hooks` flag when present. Uninstall removes Herdr entries from `hooks.json` and deletes the hook script, but leaves `config.toml` unchanged.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nIntegrations | herdr (https://herdr.dev/docs/integrations/)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn6view1\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: find({"ref_id":"turn5view1","pattern":"Codex"}); Total lines: 631\nL141: Terminal window\nL142: \nL143:     `\nL144: \nL145:     herdr integration uninstall pi\nL146: \nL147:     herdr integration uninstall omp\nL148: \nL149:     herdr integration uninstall claude\nL150: \nL151:     herdr integration uninstall codex\nL280: ## Claude Code\nL281: \nL282: \ue200cite\ue20232†Section titled “Claude Code”\ue201 L283: \nL284: Install the Claude Code hook:\nL285: \nL286: Terminal window\nL287: \nL288:     `\nL289: \nL290:     herdr integration install claude\nL291: \nL292:     `\nL293: \nL294: The hook reports Claude Code session identity to the local Herdr socket on session start. Claude Code state comes from Herdr’s screen manifest detection.\nL295: Claude integration v10 matches only the documented `SessionStart` sources: `startup`, `resume`, `clear`, `compact`, and `fork`. This prevents Grok’s `new` and `load` events from launching the imported Herdr Claude hook, without disabling unrelated Claude-compatible hooks. Reinstall the Claude integration after upgrading to migrate the previous wildcard matcher. New Claude source values require an integration update.\nL296: Herdr uses `~/.claude` by default, or `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` when set. The Claude config directory must already exist. Install writes `hooks/herdr-agent-state.sh` and updates `settings.json` with Herdr hook entries. Uninstall removes the matching hook entries and deletes the hook script.\nL297: ## Codex\nL298: \nL299: \ue200cite\ue20233†Section titled “Codex”\ue201 L300: \nL301: Install the Codex hook:\nL302: \nL303: Terminal window\nL304: \nL305:     `\nL306: \nL307:     herdr integration install codex\nL308: \nL309:     `\nL310: \nL311: The Codex hook reports session identity through the same local socket API used by other integrations. Codex state comes from Herdr’s screen manifest detection.\nL312: Herdr uses `~/.codex` by default, or `CODEX_HOME` when set. The Codex config directory must already exist. Install writes `herdr-agent-state.sh`, updates `hooks.json`, and ensures `[features] hooks = true` in `config.toml`. It also removes the deprecated top-level `codex_hooks` flag when present. Uninstall removes Herdr entries from `hooks.json` and deletes the hook script, but leaves `config.toml` unchanged.\nL313: ## GitHub Copilot CLI\nL314: \nL315: \ue200cite\ue20234†Section titled “GitHub Copilot CLI”\ue201 L316: \nL317: Install the GitHub Copilot CLI hook:\nL318: \nL319: Terminal window\nL320: \nL321:     `\nL322: \nL323:     herdr integration install copilot\nL324: \nL325:     `\nL326: \nL327: The Copilot hook reports session identity through the same local socket API used by other integrations. Copilot state comes from Herdr’s screen manifest detection.\nL328: Herdr uses `~/.copilot` by default, or `COPILOT_HOME` when set. The Copilot config directory must already exist. Install writes `hooks/herdr-agent-state.sh` and updates `settings.json` with a `SessionStart` hook entry. Uninstall removes Herdr entries from `settings.json` and deletes the hook script.\nL329: \nL330: After Copilot emits a session-bearing event, Herdr can use the reported session id to resume the pane with `copilot --resume=<id>`.\nL331: ## Devin CLI\nL332: \nL333: \ue200cite\ue20235†Section titled “Devin CLI”\ue201 L334: \nL335: Install the Devin CLI hook:\nL336: \nL337: Terminal window\nL338: \nL339:     `\nL340: \nL341:     herdr integration install devin\nL342: \nL343:     `\nL344: \nL345: The hook reports native session identity from Devin session, prompt, tool-use, permission, and stop events. Devin state still comes from Herdr’s screen manifest and OSC detection because Devin hooks do not emit a reliable state transition after every permission cancellation or user interrupt.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nSession state and restore | herdr (https://herdr.dev/docs/session-state/)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn6view2\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: find({"ref_id":"turn5view2","pattern":"detach"}); Total lines: 184\nL47: ## On this page\nL48: \nL49:   * \ue200cite\ue2020†Overview\ue201 L50:   * \ue200cite\ue20226†What survives\ue201 L51:   * \ue200cite\ue20227†Live persistence\ue201 L52:   * \ue200cite\ue20228†Snapshot restore\ue201 L53:   * \ue200cite\ue20229†Pane screen history replay\ue201 L54:   * \ue200cite\ue20230†Native agent session restore\ue201 L55:   * \ue200cite\ue20231†Live handoff\ue201 L56: \nL57: # Session state and restore\nL58: \nL59: Herdr uses several state paths for different situations.\nL60: ## What survives\nL61: \nL62: \ue200cite\ue20226†Section titled “What survives”\ue201 L63: Case  | Processes keep running  | Layout returns  | Recent screen returns  | Agent conversation resumes\nL64: --- | --- | --- | --- | ---\nL65: Detach and reattach  | Yes  | Yes  | Yes, from the live terminal  | Yes, because the process never stopped\nL66: Server restart  | No  | Yes  | Only with pane screen history  | Only with native agent session restore\nL67: Update without `--handoff`  | Compatible servers keep running; restart-required servers may need stop/restart  | Yes after restart  | Only with pane screen history  | Only with native agent session restore\nL68: Update with `--handoff`  | Best effort for supported running servers  | Yes  | Yes, from the live terminal if handoff succeeds  | Yes, because the process keeps running if handoff succeeds\nL69: ## Live persistence\nL70: \nL71: \ue200cite\ue20227†Section titled “Live persistence”\ue201 L72: \nL73: Normal detach keeps the Herdr server running. Panes, shells, agents, servers, tests, and command processes keep running inside that server.\nL74: \nL75: Detach the client with `ctrl+b q`. Reattach later:\nL76: \nL77: Terminal window\nL78: \nL79:     `\nL80: \nL81:     herdr\nL82: \nL83:     `\nL84: \nL85: This is the strongest persistence path because the original processes never stop.\nL86: ## Snapshot restore\nL87: \nL88: \ue200cite\ue20228†Section titled “Snapshot restore”\ue201 L89: \nL90: If the Herdr server stops and starts again, the original pane processes are gone. Herdr restores the saved session shape: workspaces, tabs, panes, cwd, layout, and focus.\nL91: \nL92: Snapshot restore does not preserve running shells, servers, tests, or arbitrary processes. Panes that cannot use a stronger restore path come back as new shells in their saved directories.\nL93: If `session.json` cannot be read or parsed, or requires a newer Herdr version, Herdr logs why loading failed. Before saving or clearing the replacement session, it preserves the original bytes in `session-backups/` beside `session.json` and logs the recovery path as `persist.backup`. A file missing at startup is checked again before the first save or clear. Healthy restores do not create recovery copies.\nL94: Herdr retains the three most recent recovery copies, pruning older copies only after a new copy is safely written. If preservation fails, autosave and shutdown leave the original untouched and log the failure; preservation is retried on the next save request. Copies are never restored automatically. To recover one, stop the affected server, copy the recovery file over its `session.json`, then restart. Recovery copies do not include pane screen history.\nL95: ## Pane screen history replay\nL96: \nL97: \ue200cite\ue20229†Section titled “Pane screen history replay”\ue201 L98: \nL99: Pane screen history restores recent terminal contents after a full server restart without restoring the old process.\nL100: \nL101: Pane screen history is off by default because pane output can include secrets, tokens, prompts, and command output. Enable it in the config file:\nL102: \nL103:     `\nL104: \nL105:     [experimental]\nL106: \nL107:     pane_history = true\nL108: \nL109:     `\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nSession state and restore | herdr (https://herdr.dev/docs/session-state/)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn6view3\ue201 handoff\ue201 L56: \nL57: # Session state and restore\nL58: \nL59: Herdr uses several state paths for different situations.\nL60: ## What survives\nL61: \nL62: \ue200cite\ue20226†Section titled “What survives”\ue201 L63: Case  | Processes keep running  | Layout returns  | Recent screen returns  | Agent conversation resumes\nL64: --- | --- | --- | --- | ---\nL65: Detach and reattach  | Yes  | Yes  | Yes, from the live terminal  | Yes, because the process never stopped\nL66: Server restart  | No  | Yes  | Only with pane screen history  | Only with native agent session restore\nL67: Update without `--handoff`  | Compatible servers keep running; restart-required servers may need stop/restart  | Yes after restart  | Only with pane screen history  | Only with native agent session restore\nL68: Update with `--handoff`  | Best effort for supported running servers  | Yes  | Yes, from the live terminal if handoff succeeds  | Yes, because the process keeps running if handoff succeeds\nL69: ## Live persistence\nL70: \nL71: \ue200cite\ue20227†Section titled “Live persistence”\ue201 L72: \nL73: Normal detach keeps the Herdr server running. Panes, shells, agents, servers, tests, and command processes keep running inside that server.\nL74: \nL75: Detach the client with `ctrl+b q`. Reattach later:\nL76: \nL77: Terminal window\nL78: \nL79:     `\nL80: \nL81:     herdr\nL82: \nL83:     `\nL84: \nL85: This is the strongest persistence path because the original processes never stop.\nL86: ## Snapshot restore\nL87: \nL88: \ue200cite\ue20228†Section titled “Snapshot restore”\ue201 L89: \nL90: If the Herdr server stops and starts again, the original pane processes are gone. Herdr restores the saved session shape: workspaces, tabs, panes, cwd, layout, and focus.\nL91: \nL92: Snapshot restore does not preserve running shells, servers, tests, or arbitrary processes. Panes that cannot use a stronger restore path come back as new shells in their saved directories.\nL93: If `session.json` cannot be read or parsed, or requires a newer Herdr version, Herdr logs why loading failed. Before saving or clearing the replacement session, it preserves the original bytes in `session-backups/` beside `session.json` and logs the recovery path as `persist.backup`. A file missing at startup is checked again before the first save or clear. Healthy restores do not create recovery copies.\nL94: Herdr retains the three most recent recovery copies, pruning older copies only after a new copy is safely written. If preservation fails, autosave and shutdown leave the original untouched and log the failure; preservation is retried on the next save request. Copies are never restored automatically. To recover one, stop the affected server, copy the recovery file over its `session.json`, then restart. Recovery copies do not include pane screen history.\nL95: ## Pane screen history replay\nL96: \nL97: \ue200cite\ue20229†Section titled “Pane screen history replay”\ue201 L98: \nL99: Pane screen history restores recent terminal contents after a full server restart without restoring the old process.\nL100: \nL101: Pane screen history is off by default because pane output can include secrets, tokens, prompts, and command output. Enable it in the config file:\nL102: \nL103:     `\nL104: \nL105:     [experimental]\nL106: \nL107:     pane_history = true\nL108: \nL109:     `\nL110: When enabled, Herdr stores saved pane history in `session-history.json` next to `session.json`. Treat the Herdr config/session directory like terminal history.\nL111: ## Native agent session restore\nL112: \nL113: \ue200cite\ue20230†Section titled “Native agent session restore”\ue201 L114: \nL115: Some agents can resume their own conversation sessions. Herdr can use official integration-reported session references to restart supported agent panes after a Herdr server restart.\nL116: \nL117: Native agent session restore is enabled by default. Disable it with:\nL118: \nL119:     `\nL120: \nL121:     [session]\nL122: \nL123:     resume_agents_on_restore = false\nL124: \nL125:     `\nL126: Herdr only resumes panes that reported a native session reference through a current official Herdr integration.\nL127: \nL128: After a client attaches and provides terminal size and theme context, Herdr resumes eligible restored agent panes across workspaces and tabs without waiting for each pane to be focused.\nL129: \nL130: Native session restore requires these Herdr integration versions or newer:\nL131: Agent  | Minimum Herdr integration version  | Resume command\nL132: --- | --- | ---\nL133: Pi  | `2`  | `pi --session <path-or-id>`\nL134: Antigravity CLI  | `1`  | `agy --conversation <id>`\nL135: OMP  | `3`  | `omp --resume=<path-or-id>`\nL136: Claude Code  | `6`  | `claude --resume <id>`\nL137: Codex  | `5`  | `codex resume <id>`\nL138: Cursor Agent CLI  | `1`  | `cursor-agent --resume <id>`\nL139: Grok CLI  | `2`  | `grok --resume <id>`\nL140: GitHub Copilot CLI  | `2`  | `copilot --resume=<id>`\nL141: Devin CLI  | `2`  | `devin --resume <id>`\nL142: Droid  | `2`  | `droid --resume <id>`\nL143: Kimi Code CLI  | `3`  | `kimi --session <id>`\nL144: Qoder CLI  | `2`  | `qodercli --resume <id>`\nL145: Qwen Code  | `1`  | `qwen --resume <id>`\nL146: Letta Code  | `1`  | `letta --conversation <id>` or `letta --conversation default --agent <agent-id>` for `default:<agent-id>`\nL147: OpenCode  | `5`  | `opencode --session <id>`\nL148: Kilo Code CLI  | `1`  | `kilo --session <id>`\nL149: Hermes Agent  | `2`  | `hermes --resume <id>`\nL150: MastraCode  | `1`  | `mastracode --thread <id>`\nL151: Run `herdr integration status` to check installed integration versions. Reinstall outdated integrations with `herdr integration install <agent>`.\nL152: \nL153: Unsupported, missing, invalid, duplicated, or stale session references restore as normal shells in the saved pane directory.\nL154: \nL155: If native agent session restore applies to a pane, Herdr resumes the agent session instead of replaying saved pane history for that pane.\nL156: ## Live handoff\nL157: \nL158: \ue200cite\ue20231†Section titled “Live handoff”\ue201 L159: \nL160: Live handoff is for update and remote attach flows that need to replace a running Herdr server. It asks the old server to transfer live panes to the new server, so pane processes can keep running across the server replacement.\nL161: \nL162: Unlike snapshot restore, pane history replay, and native agent session restore, handoff tries to keep the current processes alive rather than reconstructing state after the old server stops.\nL163: A successful handoff preserves long-lived server-owned session state: pane PTYs and processes, agent identity and durable metadata, and plugin/session state needed by the replacement server. It does not preserve transient coordination across the replacement boundary. In-flight CLI or API requests, waits, subscription streams, client sockets, and pane-to-pane messages may be interrupted; clients should reconnect and retry them.\nL164: Updated Unix servers can send more than 64 panes in one handoff by transferring them in batches. The sending server must already contain this support: an older running server still enforces its 64-pane limit on the first upgrade. That limit may require a normal server restart, which ends pane processes. Batching does not change update installation order or remove other handoff compatibility requirements.\nL165: \nL166: Live handoff is experimental and opt-in:\n'}]

## assistant

You are configured correctly for what the source actually told us to set up.

What the video required:

- Install Herdr and run `herdr`.
- Start Claude and Codex inside its panes.
- Use one workspace per project, tabs for activities, and panes for agents or a dev server.
- Install the Herdr skill so agents can create panes and expose their collaboration.
- Use the sidebar to watch agent status.
- Detach with `Ctrl+B`, then `Q`; reopening `herdr` reconnects without stopping active work.
- Split a pane with `Ctrl+B`, then `V`, or use the right-click menu.

What it did not prescribe:

- No specific theme, sound, toast, label, experiment, or keybinding settings.
- No plugin was required.
- No custom config file was required.

Verified on your machine:

- The screenshot shows the intended Claude-left, Codex-right layout and both agents in the sidebar.
- Claude and Codex have active Herdr hooks for session restore.
- The Herdr skill is installed for both Claude and Codex.
- Herdr onboarding is complete.
- Native agent-session restore is enabled by default, and your integration versions exceed the required minimums. [Herdr integrations](https://herdr.dev/docs/integrations/), [session persistence](https://herdr.dev/docs/session-state/).

Your Herdr config intentionally contains only:

```toml
onboarding = false
```

That is correct. Herdr’s own documentation says it works without a custom config file; custom themes, sounds, notifications, and keyboard overrides are optional preferences. [Configuration docs](https://herdr.dev/docs/configuration/)

One distinction: Claude’s “Auto mode” notice in the left pane is a Claude Code permission setting, not a Herdr setting. The video did not recommend changing it.
